ParlaMint-AT_1996-03-13-020-XX-NRSITZ-00010_d7e5956	The Federal Minister for Science, Research and Art has now been reported to answer the question in question.
ParlaMint-AT_1996-03-13-020-XX-NRSITZ-00010_d7e5978	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! High house! Before addressing the individual questions, let me make a few fundamental comments. I think that in a difficult situation, in a parliamentary debate in the context of an urgent question, there is no doubt that there is room and also legitimacy for a very free dealing with realities. However, I believe, in view of the seriousness of the situation, that the obligation to the truth should be given to a somewhat more precise extent. I would just like to mention a few points here. If it has been said that 95 percent of the students are affected by the family allowance package, you need to know that not even 50 percent of students receive family allowances. It was said that 33 to 40 percent of the income loss for the assistants occurred. I only got a calculation from the particularly concerned economic university in the afternoon that this loss of income for someone with a starting content and a relatively high number of teaching assignments – a case where a lot comes together – is 11 percent. And this without any use of offered coatings. So 11 percent are against this claim 33 to 40 percent. There are also figures such as 70 000 S to 100 000 S of a number of 41 000 S. It is completely wrong to say that universities would not have or would not have had autonomous spaces here. On the contrary: It has been offered re-transmission possibilities, which literally go to the border of what is permitted by budgetary law, and far beyond what has so far existed in autonomous design possibilities. It is also wrong to say that we are now introducing uneven rates of tuition. The main problem we have, apart from the need to participate in a consolidation package, is that, at present, the teaching rates are incredibly unfairly distributed. I assume that you also know that for one and the same teaching activity it has nothing to do with the quality of the event, whether someone has received a rehabilitated or a nonrehabilitated teaching assignment – and that is currently in a ratio of 1 : 4. That is, the claim that this novelle introduces uneven payment for the same performance is simply false – compared to what is currently in place. It was just one of the main reasons for this measure – apart from the need for consolidation, I am not talking about this point – to get away from the current incredibly unfair system. You have not rightly appealed to those who are less or perhaps not affected by your call. In fact, it is a large number less or not affected, namely those who have experienced worse treatment in the previous system, which have been worse. However, they try to give the impression that an unjust system would, so to speak, be replaced by a righteous one. It is true, rather, that the present inexplicable and unjust. I believe that, despite the need to consolidate the budget, which is undoubtedly an unpleasant exercise, we can take a structural step in the right direction. It is also not true that teaching contracts are deleted as a result of these measures. The opposite is true: Our aim throughout the negotiations was to reach an agreement that would make the contribution to consolidation possible without the need to withdraw teaching contracts, such as the provision of teaching courses. It is therefore the wrong way to assume that the number of courses has to be reduced. They also call very wrong group sizes. It is a draft law in the final stage of preparation – it will then be submitted to the Council of Ministers for decision-making; it is also accessible – in which quite different group sizes than those mentioned by you are included; but then I come to speak in detail in my reply to the question. For example, an absurd and untrue point is your finding with regard to the karences. It is quite an exception to what was not welcomed by me – and I admit that – and to universities a special stop on admission, because it was expressly entered in the Council of Ministers' decision that the post-retirement of car-retirement posts is not affected by this stop-up. This was done by us. Trusting to say here that this is a measure, particularly in the field of carers' posts, is incredible – if I do not have any other presumptions. It's just untrue! It is also quite absurd to assume – you will be able to check this yourself if the establishment plan for 1996 and for 1997 is presented – that the administration will increase in the ministry. We will, of course, be in the central office, say in the ministry, unlike in the universities – we are trying to make as much of the necessary personnel savings as possible in such a way that the universities are not affected – to realize the necessary personnel decline – without any means of doing so. There will be fewer staff in the ministry within the framework of this establishment plan – as already in 1995. It is also untrue to say that the research funds are now going back. It's exactly the opposite! They will then see in the budget – which is no longer a secret, but it has already been mentioned more often – that the funds available for research funding, especially for the FWF, for the fund for the promotion of scientific research, have increased by even around 10 percent. That is a remarkable fact in times like this. I think that in view of the genuine seriousness of the situation, it is a matter of reversing the sign as well. I also believe that it is the wrong way today to speculate precisely on what we are trying to avoid in argumentation in a negative form – or in a rhetorical way – both for the middle and for the students. That's what I call speculating with the arguments: They negate them each, but then bring them in. I think it is very necessary – I have always thought and will continue to do so – that we do not hold this debate by serving, so to speak, slightly inflammable prejudices. Whether we hate them or deny them, the name brings them to the tapet. I have not participated in this discussion and will not do so in the future. I am firmly convinced that this does not mean that the students, as well as the assistants, are being treated unjustly, and that this discussion and the attempt to put this into play by negation will also change. Of course, the budget should provide the best possible training for students. This was also our goal: that the teaching contracts in their number do not go back to the fact that the teaching offer cannot be reduced, that the whole can be safeguarded unhindered and fully budgeted. It is also true that, if you want it, strategicly important resources for equipment, not only have not been withdrawn, but have even been increased, for investments in the university sector. It is also true that we have budgeted for the start-up of the new premises, which were of great concern. On the other hand, it is not right to pretend as if the scholarships – that sounded like it; so in the meantime one not only reads such things between the lines, but also hears them between the words – only a few very poor ones. Around 40 percent of students receive family allowances, less than half of them. I do not claim that this is not a serious situation. Yet, the truth must remain nominal. So, as I said, Around 40 percent of students receive family allowances. In other words, 60 percent of students are not at all affected by this measure, but can theoretically not be affected by it – apart from the fact that it is not Question of my place of business, but that of the family ministry, but I'd like to give the information. – Of the approximately 40 percent of family aid beneficiaries, about one third receive funding. That is to say, we are talking about a third of the theoretical stakeholders who are compensated for a large part of the measures referred to by the Studienförderungsgesetz, namely scholarships. To do so – which would be socially cynical, but I do not want to go into it now – is to pretend that the Studienförderungsgesetz would cover a very small group of the poorest, is a false representation of the facts. True is: It is one third of the theoretically affected by these measures! Again – I say that again and again: In my argument, I am not going to reduce the seriousness and the effects of these measures, but I would just like to stress them by dealing with this subject in a serious way. I can now come to the answer to the individual questions: Question No 1 concerns the scope of autonomy already mentioned. It is not true that we have conjured the autonomy of the universities, as we have been told in the question, but we have provided for the legal conditions of this autonomy. But there is one point – apart from the natural involvement of universities in our legal system; this also applies to budgetary law – in which I would like to propose and become an absolute barrier, because I consider this to be justified: If, in a university, the group dynamics of opinion-building result that the funds provided by the taxpayer or by the budget or this House and by all those involved in this decision are deducted from investments in order to raise charges, I think this is wrong. That is to say, the separation between the expenditure on property and, in particular, investment loans and the staff budget is not only a restriction of autonomy, as if they could not be better, but an absolutely right measure to ensure and guarantee the funds for future equipment or for future investments. Moreover, the universities have been offered much more re-transmission opportunities within the framework of the staff budget; I have previously mentioned the number which results without re-transmission. With repainting, the income loss of the assistants is reduced by around a third again, i.e. by about a third of the 11 percent again. This is still a lot, still a serious problem, still something about which one does not follow the motto: It's not that bad! It's supposed to go over. Only: These numbers, which are serious enough, then to link with 40, 50, 60 percent wrong, I think is an unsavory process. Question No 2 concerns assessment. It was the same for all budget accompanying laws. The fact that a longer discussion would be desirable in itself is undisputed. However, we are faced with the need to introduce the budget accompanying legislation in parallel with the Federal budget here in the House, and we therefore had to comply with this measure, which is also applicable to all other ministries. Now for the multiple load: It is certainly true that public officials who are paid out of the budget make a special contribution to fiscal consolidation. In principle, they are all twofold affected. In addition, group-specific measures – not least in the teacher sector, where the mass will have an impact on the overtime rate – are very likely to exist in different professional groups, ensuring a parity, a balance also on the measures we had to take. I try – I repeat – not to play down the measures, but it is not that there has been, as it were, a unique situation, which does not lead to similar effects in comparable professional groups. I would like to read Question No 4, because it concerns exactly what I said earlier: "What professional groups, apart from academic medium-sized projects, are known to you, which through the savings package have to take a earnings rate of between 20 and 40 percent?" – Unkindly said: none – not even these! Question No 5 concerns the hierarchies you have referred to. The fact is that at present the Hierarchies are unfounded, partly related to the individual institutions and their history, depending on the rates on which one receives the teaching work. I think that's wrong! What we have introduced – and you have correctly described this – is a bill of teaching, in fact, according to habilitated and non-habilitated, because, as you probably know, it is in the draft that all currently employed – this "currently" is valid until September of this year and for their entire duration of employment as a university assistant – that the non-promovators can participate in the self-employed teaching, at the same rates and at the same conditions as those for doctoral assistants. Question 6 concerns the first hour of university lecturers. The structure of the hourly rates of university lecturers is such that, as you yourself have described, they are higher than those of the others. It therefore seemed justified to use this first hour freely. In the case of a total number of hours corresponding to the average teaching activity, however, this is by far balanced by the higher rates. Question 7 concerns the distribution to the professors. The change in the tax of exams is primarily at the expense of the professors, so that the overall distribution is very likely to achieve a balance. In addition, the rate of pay per hour after the collegiate allowance is much lower than the rate currently applied on the reclaimed teaching contracts. At the same time, I would like to say this, we have agreed with the trade union that, without having to deal with the next package of savings now, the collegiate money discussion has to be conducted very well in the context of a more profound analysis of the status, because, as some probably know here in the room, the supplements to the collegiate money are also objectively difficult to justify in this form. However, for this measure no longer applies that it can be placed under the pre-text of income neutrality. The same applies to the proposal to set up degressive audit taxes. On the question 8, which concerns the art universities: First of all, most of the courses are held at the art universities of professors and federal teachers and, of course, the teaching activities of the assistants. In the last two years, almost all long-term teachers with a high hourly scale in the framework of the lecturer's action in the Federal Teacher's Status have been taken over into a federal teacher's service relationship. The share of teaching assignments in the entire course offer has thus fallen significantly, and therefore we do not expect there to be significant changes. Furthermore, when taking over a teaching contract by an external person, social security continues to exist in the teaching contract, and for many – especially for freelance artists – this is an essential incentive or not just a side effect that is connected with social security. Nothing changes either. I can answer questions 9 to 13 together: The group numbers you mentioned are not true. The draft federal law on the remuneration states that in the future, if held by external teachers, at least 15 students will have to participate throughout the course. A course fee is only made after this draft law, if at least ten students participate in it throughout the course. In the medical subjects, mandatory facilities and "beside teaching" can also take place in micro-groups. This refers to your formulation: It is not reasonable for patients to be tested at the same time by 10 or 15 students. So it's just not true what's going on in your questions or even in your speech. The answer is simple: As before, there are three for the non-remunerated and five for the remunerated. The number 3 corresponds to the current legal situation. The minimum number of participants of 5 for Remunerated but probably not compulsory training courses. 10 to 15 participants are only provided for as a minimum size for courses which are not compulsory courses. So while you are constantly talking about 15 and 10 in your question, it applies to mandatory events 3 and 5. 10 and 15 shall apply to non-compulsory events. The minimum number of participants also does not refer to the admissibility of holding the course, but very likely to refer to the question of remuneration. Question 14, I think, has already been answered. – For all non-promovated persons, the right to independent teaching will remain for the entire duration of their employment. The question 15 essentially relates to the family compensation law. I am not responsible for this question, as has been said. However, I was very likely to make a correction here on the funding of studies. According to § 2 AHStG, university teachers have to assess the teaching material and the courses in such a way that students can complete their studies within the planned duration. The rules on compliance with the study period in the Family Tax Compensation Act comply with the requirements of the Studienförderungsgesetz (Study Promotion Act) for decades. If compliance with the duration of the claim is impossible for reasons which a student does not have to represent, the duration of the claim will be extended both for family support and for student support. So the often mentioned examples of what can happen, for which the student can't do anything, which then results in the duration of the claim being extended, are recognised grounds for interruption. These do not lead to the loss of family aid. Nor is the average duration of study for all students, but the possibility for full-time students to complete their studies within the planned duration of the qualification. It is up to the university institutions to ensure that the requirements of content are fulfilled by students who do so seriously within the planned period of study. The new study law, which can hopefully be debated here in the House before the summer, at least in the Committee, is aimed at reducing de facto study periods. The current study period exceeded – on question 16 – amounts to about five semesters. However, there are all students involved, including those who are fully employed and who, according to experience, cover far more semesters than the average student. The professionals are also a strongly increasing group of students. The main reason for the overrun is the employment. There is – again: Thank God! – a strongly increasing group of professionals who are starting to study. It is clear that those who are not affected by the removal of the family allowance have a much longer study period than those of the average. This is also something I have always represented, namely that there must be no negative consequences on it. Again: Furthermore, we want to significantly reduce study times by means of the new study law. On the question 18: I do not want to repeat now as far as investment funds are concerned. These have also made it possible in recent years to substantially expand the facilities. And if I say to you that since 1990, there have been some 3 500 additional posts for universities, then it is likely that this is a measure which can fix the conditions described by you. At the same time, it is indisputable that work to improve the study conditions should, as it were, be regarded as incomplete. On question 19: In recent years, the average duration of study has changed solely through new legal requirements, from 13.1 to 13.9 semesters. There are also all those who, because they are working, study much longer. It must also be said that the new study law will now lead to corrections, namely the de facto study period. On question 20, the average age of the initials. That's an interesting point. If you take the first degree, i.e. those students who complete a study – their first degree, so to speak – up to and including 27, then 63 percent of all students under 27 years of age, with an average age of 25 years. If you increase this to: How many close below 30?, then the average age increases from 25 to 26 years, and that already covers 85 percent of students. That means, in other words, 85 percent of students leave earlier than 30 years, on average with 26, and 63 percent of students leave below 27, on average with 25. Question 21 concerns the stopover. I've already said: The care centres are not affected and the medical faculties are not affected. – This does not change the fact that this is a difficult measure for universities, which I hope they will soon end. Questions 22 and 23: We have provided that through the Studienförderungsgesetz, there will be a possibility for the former and also future scholarship recipients to compensate for a large part of what they may have to do – I am no matter why I say "probably" – in losses. "Maybe" I say so because one has to distinguish very precisely between those who live relatively close to their university and for whom the disappearance of the network card probably does not represent such social hardness, and those who may live and commute outside the study location. We have agreed with the ÖH that we will create our own fund, which will cover 4.5% of the funding for study funding and will be proposed in the amendment to the Studienförderungsgesetz as a budget accompanying law. With this 4.5 percent – which is just over 50 million schilling – we will achieve a balance, but targeting only those who are particularly affected. As regards R & D, i.e. research expenses and academics: First of all, the proportion of the current final visavis of the respective equal age group is, of course, already much higher than the average figures in the request here, which shows the state and transmits statistically into figures which everyone knows that the number of students has increased considerably in recent years. In this way, the average number of people in need is increasing. The second point is that we have a system through the higher education schools in Austria, which I consider to be perfectly correct and high-quality, but which leads to many going directly to the economy after completion of this training, in particular HTL graduates, and not going to study. I do not consider it a disadvantage for education policy to do so. This is an institution, an educational path that has become almost unique in Europe. I mean "unique" in the positive sense, but that's why these comparisons already suffer a little. In addition to this, you know that the high-school sector has been introduced, because we had a manco in the non-university field, which can now be successfully compensated for through the colleges. As far as research expenditure is concerned, the resources that came from the ministry will have increased from 1.2 billion in 1990 to 2.8 billion in 1996. This is an increase of 133 percent within six years, so that's probably not so bad. At the same time, it is true that we have dealt particularly with the funds, as I have already described, but also the Academy of Sciences here. In particular, the funds benefit the assistants to a large extent, and 95 percent of the funds go to projects proposed by universities. ParlaMint-AT_1996-03-13-020-XX-NRSITZ-00010_d7e11419	Mr President. Mr. Federal Minister! High house! Today's labour market policy law 1996 is the first of a number of laws to be passed on to Austrians under the title of tax package as social changes. The basic intensification of this social law is correct: older workers who have become unemployed, who are long-term unemployed, return to working life. But as always, the devil is hidden in detail. The so-called bonus malus system, as it is foreseen, has not found the approval of our liberals in far-reaching points, which is what the Malus is about to achieve. We believe that the malus is insufficiently focused and will only serve to bring down the retirement age for very, very many workers, i.e. in German: The number of older workers released – perhaps at 43 or 45 years of age for women – will thus be increased. For male workers, this age is likely to be significantly less than 50 years old. This can already be seen from the fact that the bonus is used with a maximum of about a gross monthly reference, but that after ten years of service, the finals prescribed are threefold of them. I therefore believe that this malus system is right from the basic intensification to grant dismissal protection, but in practice – and this applies to many of the first well-informed protection provisions – a counter-productive effect for workers. Federal Minister! I would ask you, from this point of view, to introduce, immediately with the implementation of this law, accompanying control by the promotion of the labour market, which is the result of this impact, because it cannot be assumed that, although the practice outlined and feared by us will occur at the expense of the workers in the future, the legislator will not immediately provide for more labour-friendly regulations regarding their employment. At the same time, I have already stated in the Committee that the attempt to redraft and re-publish the ASVG for the citizens of that country, so that it is possible for every worker to take his/her life planning, his/her entire work planning into his/her own hands, to determine it himself, to say goodbye to this practice of the Federal Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs, "Sammelnovelles". Deputies Mag. Cordula Frieserund Edeltraud Gattererof the Austrian People's Party were particularly apostrophic, because they have gone into the election campaign to ensure more readable and consolidated laws in this area. What is available here today, ladies of the ÖVP, certainly does not correspond to these intentions. If one considers what we will still be in the house for this year of changes in labour law, one must say: With the same practice, we have to expect four to five ASVG novels this year. However, I do not believe that, Mr Federal Minister, it will be appropriate to enable individual workers to better plan their lives, but rather that the outpouring ASVG will become even more expansive if it continues to do so. Even the title of this law, "Labour Market Policy Act 1996", is so inconceivable that one can hardly imagine anything underneath it if one does not want to start lengthy and pointed research into individual sources. Today I have looked at a computer expression of the Institute for Social Law of the University of Vienna, which includes the individual novels that have come to us from the adoption of the ASVG to this day. Officially, we hold 53. ASVG-Novelle. In fact, this novel, which we adopt today, is actually the 120. In 1995 alone, we adopted five novels that were hidden four times in other legal acts – and this, although they had significant effects on workers whose rights and obligations were included. Federal Minister! If you are interested in ensuring clarity and greater transparency in this regard, then we should finally address this – Professor Tomandel has already called for it in 1988 to be re-enacted on the occasion of his retirement – not only verbally to make a re-codification of social law in Austria, but indeed to do so. Such a need is more urgent than ever for those who give advice in this area. [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] The criticism of this legislative text was clearly and clearly formulated by us in the Committee, and speakers of the Liberal Group will then explain the various points on this subject, such as the concern of women, which are clearly and clearly disadvantaged by this new law. I would not like to say that I was disappointed that the debate on Amendment No 51/A by Mr Haider and Mr Dolinschek on promoting the employment of older people in the Committee was dealt with so briefly. Federal Minister! In particular, item 4 on leave, which we liberals have also provided for training measures for older workers, we believe should have more clearly discussed in the Committee. Just you spoke on Sunday three weeks ago. Lifelong learning and lifelong training. I therefore believe that the basic ideas that we have formulated in our proposal 51/A would have been quite a majority. I also believe that the idea of taking into account, for long-term unemployed, a corresponding remuneration by means of unemployment support for sub-group contract wages and thus reducing the proportion of additional labour costs and labour costs as a whole, but paying the worker the same wage as he is guaranteed by the collective contract, would have earned more than a sharp rejection, which meant: Where are we going to be if sub-group contract wages were to be taken out of the unemployed by means of compensatory payments! There are quite similar systems, such as those of the German army's time-consuming soldiers, who can spend the last year of work – when they refund all their salaries – in the economy when they are in their profession in final training. For example, bakers can prepare for master exams and work in the ninth service year of the Federal Army with a baker, of which only the overtimes can be paid, while all other costs carry the army where he is not.
